Rendered frames back that up — the restaurant diners are seated at their tables, the supermarket shopper stands at the counter. So the interiors do look fixed.

**The one that isn't: AREA 2, Anekbah Grotte Gandhar.** 16 staged, and 15 of them report at frame 0:

```
pose: actor 184 ZOH_FN - its program ended; he stays where it left him (0 0 0)
                         and falls back to the bank's idle
frame 0: actor 184 ZOH_FN - pose source: the rest pose (no bank clip)
```

That is exactly the shape you described, and it is two faults stacked: the object's program ends before reaching a body-animation step, so `s.at` is never written (it stays 0 0 0 rather than falling back to the placement record), and `ZOH_FN`/`GND_FN` carry no `.CTL` bank, so the idle fallback has no clip and `composePose` gets empty tracks — a T-pose. The 16th, `PA1_FN`, is the known model with no drawable geometry.

The cave is not a shop, so it may be a different bug from the one you saw. Want me to chase it, or leave it?

**Claude**

Committed as `f470ee5`. Here's where it stands.

## What was actually broken

`play.cpp`'s *"the program ended, so he stays where it left him"* branch carried `drawAt` over — but it also fired when **no program ever drove the body**, and on an actor's first frame `drawAt` has never been written. So any chunk-placed character no scene program names was teleported to the world origin and his 20-byte placement record thrown away. Split on a new `Staged::progRan`; the cave now stands its sixteen where the chunk puts them.

That is the "wrong place" half, and it was general — it hit all twelve affected areas.

## What the T-pose is

Your note about shoot mode is exactly it, and the code says so. Those characters carry **no `.CTL` in any of the three 9-byte slots** (136 / 92 / 141 of the 1032 records name an adventure / shoot / combat machine; the twelve `ZOH_FN` and `GND_FN` name none). A shoot-mode actor is posed from a different place — an **`.ani` library the AREA chunk names at `+124`**:

```
0x0040CA9C   chunk+124 + ".ani"       -> sub_434010
sub_434010   "ANIMS\%s", "3.0V" magic -> dword_52B95C, 24-byte records
sub_434530   the record whose +0 is the CHARACTER TYPE
Shoot_ActorEnter    that list -> shoot record +20, ACTOR_STATE 3
Shoot_ActorAction   List_PickRandomByType(list, 9 / 10 / 11)
```

with the binary naming its own failures — `"Perso %d non existant dans le .ani"`, `"anim non existante dans le .ANI"`. **20 areas name a library, 9 distinct names, all 9 ship**, and they corroborate against the models those areas stage: GANDHAR↔`ZOH_FN`/`GND_FN`, ZTECH↔`ZTK_FN`, BRAQUEUR↔`BRA_FN`, PASSANTH↔the city crowd. `zombie.ani` and `biblio.ani` are named by no area. New check `verify.py: area .ani library`, shown to fail (a 4-byte offset shift takes it to 0 of 8 names resolving).

## The sweep, all 257 areas

423 bodies pose from a scene program, 34 from the bank's default entry, **72 from the rest pose across 12 areas** — 8 of which name an `.ani`:

| area | `.ani` | T-posed |
|---|---|---|
| 2 Grotte Gandhar | GANDHAR | GND_FN, ZOH_FN ×14 |
| 61 / 71 / 77 / 80 Tetra, Docks | DOCK | MEC_FN, TEH_FN, MBK_FNM… |
| 144 Tetra 4 | ZTECH | ZTK_FN ×3 |
| 230 Supermarché 2 | BRAQUEUR | V5H_FNM, PSH_FN, FSH_FN |
| 249 Jaunpur Toits | DOCK | LB_FN |
| 35 Morgue, 68 Supermarché 1, 218 CS Sas, 237 Appart Kay'l | — | M2_FN; BRA_FN ×6; the crowd models ×20; TEL_FNM |

The last four name no library and have no `shoot.actor.enter`, so they are a separate question — 237 is Telis in the apartment, who is driven by an `Aapkayl.SCX` object that nothing in AREA 237's own scripts starts, so that one may be an artefact of jumping straight to the area rather than walking in.

Two routes from here, both a step of their own: **port the shoot pose** (the `.ani` type registry + `List_PickRandomByType`, wiring opcodes 82/84 into `interp.cpp`), or **chase the four areas that name no library**. Which do you want?

**Claude**

Here's where things stand while those finish.

**Your report reproduces, and the cause is an ordering fault in the port.**

Jumping straight to AREA 46 hid it completely — `omk-play` makes the `.SCX` resident itself before the first frame. Walking in from Anekbah (zone 10, *Porte 26*) it shows immediately:

| | walking in, before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `resto.SCX` resident | frame **145** | frame **65** |
| AREA 46 shown | frame 66 | frame 66 |
| diners on a scene clip | **0 of 8** | **8 of 8** |
| diners in the rest pose | 7 | 0 |
| actor 59 | 6716 −41 419 (his placement record) | 7277 8 556 (his path) |

The before-frame is exactly your description: six people T-posed and bunched by the door instead of seated.

**The original settles the order.** `Area_TickLoad` (0x0040C7E0):

```
case 2  Area_LoadScx(chunk+97, area)     <- the area's own .SCX
case 5  Actors_SpawnFromTables
case 6  Scene_LoadProps
case 7  Anim_Load(chunk+124)             <- the .ani library
case 9  the startup contexts
```

So the pool a startup script addresses is always the destination's. The port called `reloadScene` only from the transition state machine's `finishScene`, at the far end — so AREA 46's `+4` script (`scx.play.actor` for 65, 59, 62, 63, 69, 70, 71, 312) ran against `anekbah.SCX`, every handle missed, and each body fell back to its placement record in the rest pose. One line in `completeLoad` puts it at case 2.

**One check had encoded the old timing as its expectation.** `engine: tunnel door walk` required the door's *open* from the ACTIVE pool and its *close* from the OUTGOING one — true only because the destination's `.SCX` loaded late. The engine hands `ScriptObject_Start` the outgoing block for both, so both are now OUTGOING; I re-pointed the check at what it was built for (every `T01door01` motion comes from `Tunnel01.SCX`, both starts resolve with 0 missed) rather than which pool held the file. The door still opens, closes and collides.

New guard `engine: walk-in scene`, shown to fail on the reverted fix: `(False, 0, 7, False)` against `(True, 8, 0, True)`.

<cc-memory filenames="preloaded-cutscene-actors.md">And your point about pre-loading is recorded — a character T-posed below the floor with his shown bit set is staged later by a cutscene, not misplaced; actor 0 is indeed named by `scx.play.actor` elsewhere in the corpus, and AREA 237 parks her and three others at raw y 8300 against an address plane of 1079.</cc-memory>

I'll commit once the regression sweep and the engine checks report.

## The bug you found

Walking from Anekbah into the restaurant, AREA 46 came up at frame 66 but `resto.SCX` only became resident at frame **145**. Its `+4` startup script — the seven `scx.play.actor` calls for the diners, plus the waitress and Samyaza — therefore ran against `anekbah.SCX`. Every object handle missed, no program started, and all eight bodies stayed on their 20-byte placement records in the rest pose, which for the `PSH`/`FSH` crowd models is a T-pose. Six of them bunched by the door, exactly your report.

Your instruction to enter from outside is what exposed it: `--area 46` makes the `.SCX` resident before the first frame, so every earlier test of mine went down a path that could not fail.

## The fix

`Area_TickLoad` (0x0040C7E0) settles the order — case 2 `Area_LoadScx(chunk+97)`, cases 5/6 the actors and props, case 7 `Anim_Load(chunk+124)`, case 9 the startup contexts. The pool a startup script addresses is always the destination's. The port only called `reloadScene` from the transition state machine's far end; `Session::completeLoad` now does case 2 first.

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `resto.SCX` resident (walking in) | frame 145 | frame 65 |
| diners on a scene clip / rest pose | 0 / 7 | 8 / 0 |
| actor 59 | 6716 −41 419 (record) | 7277 8 556 (path) |
| 257-area sweep, bodies on a clip | 423 | **448** |

Rest-pose count unchanged at 72, bank idles at 34, 0 "nothing says WHERE" — so nothing regressed, and 25 more bodies get their programs even on the jump-in path.

`engine: tunnel door walk` had encoded the old timing (open from the ACTIVE pool, close from the OUTGOING one) — true only because the destination loaded late. The engine hands `ScriptObject_Start` the outgoing block for both, so I re-pointed the check at the motion and the resolution rather than the pool label. 20 engine checks green.

New guard `engine: walk-in scene`, shown to fail on the reverted fix: `(False, 0, 7, False)` vs `(True, 8, 0, True)`.

Two of the three are fixed with the binary as evidence. While the checks run, here's what each turned out to be.

**1. The waiter walking backward** — `Anim_RootDelta` (0x004711D0) takes `node+156` as its second argument, `Actor_LoadModel` binds that to `actor+288`, and `Actors_TickAll` rebuilds it every frame from the actor's Euler:

```c
Matrix3x3_FromEulerAngles(a[104]*rad, a[105]*rad, a[106]*rad, a + 288);
```

and the Euler is what `Script_SelectRelativeBodyAnimation` writes with `Actor_SetEuler(node, p4, p5, p6)` on the tick *before* `Actor_MoveBy(node, delta)`. So the clip's root motion is turned by the body's facing. The port turned the body and not the motion — `Serveur00` carries Euler y **145°**, so he played a walk cycle facing one way and travelled another. The Euler is also *sticky*: `Script_SelectBodyAnimation` never writes one, so his ABS walk clips travel under the 145° his preceding wait left in the record.

**2. The heads at 90°** — you were right to say check it in code. `Actor_SetHeadLook` clamps yaw to **±70°** and pitch to ±40°, so 90° can't come from that path at all. The port's `aimHead` clamps identically — but it backed the target into the body's local frame using `s.facing`, the *placement record's* facing, which is 0 for every diner, while the body is actually turned by the call's Euler of 90°. So the aim sat 90° out and pinned at the clamp:

| actor | before | after | hand-check |
|---|---|---|---|
| 71 `Pass05` | **70°** (pinned) | −14° | ~−13° |
| 63 `Pass02` | **70°** (pinned) | −7° | — |

These are zone-driven (`character.look_at_player 63` / `71` on enter, `look_away` on leave), so they only turn while you're at that table — which is correct, and away from the zone the twist is 0.

**3. The seated character** — that's Samyaza (`SAM_FN`, actor 312, object `1_Samya.Std`, clip `INC_AN01` on path `SaBas.Inc1`, Euler 180). The root-delta fix moves her by 37 units: her placement is z 713, she drifted to z **732** before and now settles at z **695** — about a seat's width, and the direction the Euler 180 demands. Whether that actually seats her I haven't confirmed by eye yet; I kept missing her with the camera.

One correction to my own earlier read: I first thought the body rotation itself was wrong, from a pair of small frames. Zoomed in, the current `+yaw` is plainly right — both diners face the table with their hands on it — so only the *motion* and the *aim frame* were wrong, not the body.

**Claude**

Committed as `4980ab9`. Two of the three are fixed with the binary as evidence; the third is moved but not confirmed.

## 1. The waiter walking backward — fixed

`Script_SelectRelativeBodyAnimation` writes `Actor_SetEuler(node, p4, p5, p6)` and *then* `Actor_MoveBy(node, delta)`, and the order is the whole point:

```
Actors_TickAll   Matrix3x3_FromEulerAngles(a[104], a[105], a[106], a+288)
Actor_LoadModel  sub_437140(node, actor + 288)          -- node+156
Anim_SetFrame    Anim_RootDelta(clip, node+156, prev, cur, out)
Anim_RootDelta   out.x = m0·dx + m3·dy + m6·dz  (row-vector), etc.
```

So a scene clip's root motion travels where the call's Euler points. The port turned the body and not the motion. The Euler is also **sticky** — `Script_SelectBodyAnimation` writes none — so `Serveur00`, Euler y 145 on its waits, walks its ABS clips under that 145.

This closes CLAUDE.md §6's open question: `Anim_RootDelta`'s 3×3 is the *same* matrix on the scene path as on the actor path; the `sub_437140(node, 0)` sites are the ones that clear it.

## 2. The heads — fixed, and you were right to send me to the code

`Actor_SetHeadLook` clamps yaw to **±70°** and pitch to ±40°, so 90° can't come from that path at all. The port clamps identically — but it backed the target into the body's local frame using the *placement record's* facing, which is 0 for every diner, while the body is turned by the call's Euler of 90°. The aim sat 90° out and pinned on the clamp.

| | before | after | hand-check |
|---|---|---|---|
| diner 63 `Pass02` | 70° (pinned) | −7° | — |
| diner 71 `Pass05` | 70° (pinned) | −14° | ~−13° |

They're zone-driven (`character.look_at_player` on enter, `look_away` on leave), so they glance only while you're at that table — correct — and read 0 away from it.

## 3. The seated character — moved, not confirmed

Samyaza (`SAM_FN`, actor 312, clip `INC_AN01` on path `SaBas.Inc1`, Euler 180). The root-delta fix swings her **37 units**, z 732 → 695 — about a seat's width, in the direction her Euler 180 demands. That may seat her, but I kept missing her with the camera and have not verified it by eye. If you can grab a frame of her on the new build I'll know straight away whether she's on the stool or still needs the get-up animation you suspected.

**A correction to my own earlier message:** I first said the body rotation itself was wrong, from a pair of small frames. Zoomed in, the current `+yaw` is plainly right — both diners face the table with their hands on it — so only the motion and the aim frame were wrong. Only the zoom settled it.

12 regression checks pass. New guard `engine: scene facing`, both halves shown to fail — the head by mutating the frame back, the motion by the check's own `OMK_NO_ROOTSPIN`.

> `Bash` — What happened in the demo session


**Claude**

The demo ran and you walked in — `1 areas entered`, ending at 6845 691 inside the restaurant, 497 units walked, clean exit. The restaurant read correctly throughout: all eight diners on scene-program clips, the waiter at 7213 −9 748, Samyaza at the fixed z 695.

But the log shows something I should flag. Let me confirm it.

> `Bash` — Does the pose lookup read both pools


**Claude**

**Confirmed — a defect the demo run exposed.** The viewer's *motion* code reads both resident scene pools (`for (sr : {&session.sceneOut(), &session.scene()})`, play.cpp:3326 — that's the tunnel-door fix), but the *pose* code reads only the active one (`session.scene()`, play.cpp:5644). So the moment the `.SCX` swaps, every body driven by the outgoing scene's programs drops to the rest pose.

Your log shows it: at exit, the restaurant's eight are on scene-program clips while **seventeen of Anekbah's street crowd** — actors 72, 459–464, 50, 61, 465–469 — read `the rest pose (no bank clip)`, because `anekbah.SCX` is now the outgoing pool.

Two honest caveats:

- This is **not new** — it predates today's work. What my case-2 fix changed is *when*: the swap now happens as the area loads (frame 65) rather than at the end of the transition (frame 145), so the outgoing crowd freezes ~80 frames earlier, during the window where both sets are still drawn.
- In your walk it was probably invisible, since Anekbah was behind you as you went through the door. Turning around mid-doorway, or any transition where the outgoing area stays in view, would show a street of T-poses.

The fix is the same shape as the door one: search both pools for the program that names an actor, which also matches the engine — the port's own comment already records that a transition doesn't rebuild the object pool and the outgoing programs go on running.

Want me to do that now, or would you rather I wait for what you saw in the demo — particularly whether Samyaza is on her stool?

**Claude**

You were right that it was mirrored — but the mirror was a symptom, not the fault. Here's what it turned out to be, while the checks run.

**My last commit had it wrong.** I claimed the Euler was *sticky* — that only `Script_SelectRelativeBodyAnimation` writes it, so an alternating program carries the last one. That came from the FILE_FORMATS transcription of `Script_SelectBodyAnimation`, which lists only its *position* half. Its tail is the same three lines as the relative call's:

```c
Anim_SetFrame (node, clip, prev, cur, delta);
Actor_SetEuler(node, p4, p5, p6);
Actor_MoveBy  (node, delta[0], delta[1], delta[2]);
```

So **every** body-animation step writes the Euler, and a step that authors 0 turns the actor back to 0. `Serveur00` is Euler y **145** on his standing waits and **0** on his `SERV00`/`01`/`02` walk clips. The port carried 145 into the walks and rotated his entire route by it — through the walls, and yanked back at every hand-over, which is exactly the teleporting you added.

**The invariant that settles it** — a program's steps are authored to chain, so a correct run re-places the body on the spot it already occupies:

| | mean gap | worst | over 100 units |
|---|---|---|---|
| Euler per step | **0** | **2** | 0 of 68 |
| Euler as sticky | 28 | **628** | several |

**And what did *not* decide it, which is the lesson.** I spent two rounds testing +yaw against −yaw against no-yaw, by walk mesh and by hand-over gap, and neither separated them — because the assembly is unambiguous about the sense: `Matrix3x3_FromEulerAngles` with pitch and roll zero is `[[cy,0,sy],[0,1,0],[-sy,0,cy]]`, and `Anim_RootDelta`'s row-vector tail is exactly `rotateYaw(+yaw)`. A character walking through a wall was a symptom to locate, not a verdict to accept — and what it located was *which calls write the Euler*.

Also picked up: the absolute call's own offset (params 7/8/9 against the clip root, where the relative call uses 9/10/11 against the path). Zero at every site seen so far, but it's read now.

`engine: scene facing` now carries the hand-over invariant, with `OMK_STICKY_EULER` as the mutation that takes the worst gap past 200. I'll commit once the regression run reports.

The per-actor lookup now walks `{&scene(), &sceneOut()}`, active pool first so a body both could claim keeps the incoming scene's program. `OMK_ACTIVE_POOL_ONLY` is the mutation, folded into `engine: walk-in scene`.

**And I've located the camera mechanism** (item 4) while that ran, which is worth reporting now because it changes the shape of the work:

`sub_413C00` — the ordinary adventure follow camera — sets `flags |= 0x1C`, that is **4 | 8 | 0x10**. In the camera tick `sub_417CF0`, flag 4 opens the collision branch, 0x10 runs `sub_416450` first, and flag 8 selects `sub_417070` as the arm. So the engine *does* have a camera collision pass, and it is `sub_417070` (271 lines).

Its first stage, transcribed:

```
D = eye − target, rescaled to |D| × (+300)          ; +300 is 1.2
P = target + D                                       ; 1.2× the eye distance
sub_444810(target, P, &hit, −1, &dist, &obj, …)      ; a scene ray cast
```

— a ray from the **target** out past the eye, and on a hit the eye is pulled in. `sub_413C00` also loads a set of parameters the pass uses: `+312/+316 = −0.7 × pelvis height`, `+228/+240 = +0.7 ×` it, `+300 = 1.2`, `+308 = 0.5`, `+284 = 5.0`, `+320 = 8.0`, `+324 = 4.0`, `+288 = 8`. There's a second, simpler pass, `sub_416570`, for cameras with flag 4 but not 8 — same idea, target→eye, eye moved to the hit.

One correction to something I said two messages ago: I first read `sub_413CD0` as the follow-camera setup and said it sets flag 4 unconditionally. It does, but it only runs for ACTOR_STATE 11/13/14; ordinary walking takes `sub_413C00`.

Continuing to items 2 and 3 now, then the camera itself — `sub_417070` is a real transcription job and I'd rather come to it with the smaller two out of the way.

**Item 4, the camera.** I've transcribed `sub_417070` — the arm flag 8 selects — into `PlayerController::cameraCollide`:

```
D = eye − target ;  P = target + D·1.2
if (cast target → P hits)   eye pulled to the hit, distance kept in +328
else if recovering          +328 eased back out over 8 frames
plus a LIFT of −0.7 × pelvis height blended in as it closes, eased over 4
```

It builds and is wired to the walkable + steep soups, with `OMK_NO_CAM_COLLIDE` as the mutation. **What `sub_417070` turned out to be**, and it answers your "keep the same behaviour as the original" directly — the engine does have a camera collision pass, and the adventure camera is the one that uses the elaborate arm:

```
sub_413C00   flags |= 0x1C          = 4 | 8 | 0x10
sub_417CF0   flag 4 opens the branch, 0x10 runs sub_416450,
             flag 8 selects sub_417070   (a camera with 4 but not 8 gets
                                          sub_416570, the nine-line version)
```

and `sub_417070` itself:

```
D = eye − target ;  P = target + D·1.2
hit?  → eye pulled to the hit; the distance is kept in +328
none? → +328 eased back out over 8 frames (+320)
plus a LIFT of −0.7 × pelvis height blended in as it closes,
     eased over 4 frames (+324)
```

The over-reach of 1.2 and both easing rates come from `sub_413C00`'s own constants, so this isn't a spring arm I invented.

**Measured** at three stands where the camera sat through a wall:

| stand | without | with |
|---|---|---|
| 6980, 450, 180 | dist 119, **through** | dist 74, clear |
| 7100, 600, 180 | dist 119, **through** | dist 79, clear |
| 7250, 880, 0 | dist 119, **through** | dist 9, still through |

The third pinches to 9 and stays blocked because the *target* is inside the wall there — `--stand` put the player in geometry, not a camera fault.

Three things I deliberately did **not** model, all labelled in the code rather than left to be found: the second ray of the recovery branch (it decides how fast recovery starts, not whether the camera is in a wall); flag 1, the "just changed" bit that makes the engine snap instead of ease for one frame after a camera change; and the face set — the engine's `sub_444810` walks the scene's meshes and can skip one by flag, where I cast against the walker's walkable + steep soups, so a mesh the engine would see through is solid here.

Your global `~/.gitconfig` still sets `sofiane@wixar.io`, which means **any new repo you create on this machine picks up the work identity by default**. This one only behaves because I added a local override. That's exactly how the 31 commits happened: the global default changed on 09-04 and nothing here objected.

Git has a directory-scoped mechanism for precisely this — `includeIf`, keyed on where the repo lives:

```gitconfig
# ~/.gitconfig — personal by default
[user]
    name  = Sofiane Kerrakchou
    email = sofianekerrakchou@hotmail.fr

# ...and work only under the work tree
[includeIf "gitdir:~/Work/"]
    path = ~/.gitconfig-work
```

with `~/.gitconfig-work` holding just the `[user]` block for `sofiane@wixar.io`. Whichever way round you prefer — personal default with work carved out, or the reverse — the point is that the identity follows the directory instead of being a global you have to remember.

I'd need to edit your global config to set that up, and it affects every repo on the machine, so I'll leave it to you unless you want me to. If you tell me where your work repos live I can write it.

## 49. User  ·  2026-09-05 17:41:35

ok, i have to topic in mind: skybox/fog, and graphical options of the game (visual details, anti-aliasing, crowd density, ...) and how we can implement them (first with a config file, options menu is for later). do you have a preferred order?


**Claude**

Let me check what the tree already knows about each rather than guess at the order.

> `Bash` — What is known about fog and sky

> `Bash` — Which option rows are graphical

> `Bash` — The 74 option rows


**Claude**

Row 4 is **`Affichage du ciel`** — your two topics are the same block of the options table. Let me check one more thing: how much of the fog machinery the port already carries.

> `Bash` — The port's fog bucket and the three rows


**Claude**

That settles it. **Config/options first, then sky and fog — and they're not really two topics.**

Your second topic *contains* the first: rows 3, 4, 6 and 7 of the game's own options table are clip distance, sky on/off, crowd density and level of detail. Each carries a `[read, apply]` hook pair, and the port's bucket key already computes `key |= 0x1000` (fogged) from `maxz >= farSplit`.

**Why options first, concretely:**

- **The schema isn't a design decision.** `tables/ui.json` has all 74 rows lifted and self-checking, with their labels, their exact choice values (`Très proche` = 25 … `Très loin` = 200 for clip distance) and their hook addresses. A config file that mirrors those rows is right by construction and drops into the options menu later with no redesign — build your own shape now and you'll rewrite it.
- **Crowd density is free.** Row 6's five choices are 0–4, and `omk-play` already takes `--density 0..4`; CLAUDE.md records it as "one integer awaiting options row 6". Wiring alone.
- **Sky and clip distance *are* option rows.** Implementing them before the option plumbing means writing each toggle twice.
- **The research risk is all on the fog side.** The fog block is located (`AREA +144`, `sub_41D380(area, block+144)`) but I see no evidence it's decoded, and `PORTING` states outright that **fog has no reachable evidence tier** — the captures can't validate pixel values. So fog will land as a labelled reconstruction however we sequence it, whereas the option rows are data-backed and checkable today.

**Suggested order:**

1. **Config file mirroring the 74 rows** — read at boot, values in the game's own units, applied where the port already has a knob. Density works immediately; the rest become named no-ops with a check that every row resolves.

  ```cpp
  bool placementOfFn(const ScxFunction& f, float offset[3], float euler[3]) {
      const bool rel = f.id == 0x0200002Au;
      const bool abs = f.id == 0x02000004u;
      if (!rel && !abs) return false;
      if (f.params.size() < (rel ? 12u : 10u)) return false;
      const std::size_t oi = rel ? 9u : 7u;
      for (int k = 0; k < 3; ++k) {
          offset[k] = asFloat(f.params[oi + k]) / 2.54f;
          euler[k]  = asFloat(f.params[4 + k]);
      }
      return rel;
  }
  ```

**Yes — and it is more than the profile line I quoted.** The whole 3496-byte header of `IAM\GAMES` is the settings block, and I had never named its fields.

**What the code says.** The header is the global `byte_90E180`. `sub_41F4C0` memsets all 3496 bytes and writes every field in one run — that is what fixes the layout. `Game_WriteSave` copies the block over the file's head on **every** slot save, `sub_4092A0` writes it alone, and `SaveDir_Load` reads it back behind the `OMK_SAVE` magic and a version dword at +8. One copy serves all 256 slots, so **saving a game saves your options**.

**Every field is named** by the option row whose read hook reads it — and the rows come in the same order the defaults function writes them:

| off | row | default |
|---|---|---|
| +12 | 2 Résolution (two int16) | 640 × 480 |
| +16 / +17 | 4 sky / 5 shadows | 1, 1 |
| +20 | 3 Distance de clipping | 50 |
| +24/+28/+32 | 10/11/12 the three volumes | 0 |
| +36…+49 | 13 3D sound, 15 subtitles, 16/17 difficulties, 18 combat camera, 23/24 mouse sensitivity (20, 15), 25 inverted, 27 force feedback | |
| +52 / +276 / +500 | rows 29–71 — **keyboard / mouse / joystick**, 4 × 14 × u32 each | |
| +1446 / +1447 | **6 crowd density / 7 level of detail** | |

The binding tables are the part that proves the reading: 168 values this repo had already lifted independently out of `0x004C8F90/0x004C9070/0x004C9150` into `tables/key_bindings.json`, and all 168 match in both saves. The offsets aren't fitted either — they're the globals' own addresses minus `0x90E180`, three contiguous 224-byte tables with the exe's own `0xE0` stride. (My first attempt used 280/508 and got 105 of 168; that mutation is now one of the two the check is shown to fail on.)

**The two saves differ in exactly two of the 3496 bytes**, which is what makes them evidence and not a fixed point: `+20` is 200 vs 150 — two of row 3's five clip-distance choices, against a default of 50 — and `+1446` is 4 vs 3, two of row 6's five densities. Two sessions, two settings you actually changed.

**This corrects what I told you in step 1.** I said the crowd density and the level of detail were "menu-only" because they have no `[Preferences]` key. Having no ini key isn't the same as not persisting — they're menu-only *in the ini*, and saved here. So the game keeps its options in two places, and step 2 now has to say which wins: the ini is what it boots with, the header is what the menu edits and is therefore later. `todo/options-config.md` has that table and a new step 2b.

`verify.py: settings block` added, shown to fail twice; 172 fast-tier checks, 0 failed — which also fixed the one real failure I'd left behind, `licence headers` still asserting 340 files after step 1 added three.

Three commits are local and unpushed (`118eca9`, `d5ca895`, `f8e5173`). Say the word and I'll push, then start step 2 with the two-source design.

**Claude**

Pushed, and step 2 is done and committed (`b616967`).

**The step got bigger than planned, because one option turned out to size four things.** Row 3 is in metres and the world unit is an inch, so the engine converts with 39.37 and hands `D` to `sub_440BE0(scene, D, 1)`:

| field | value | who reads it |
|---|---|---|
| `+340` | `D` | `dword_6A2B9C` → `sub_48D3B0`'s visible-set radius, the four side planes — **and `FOGEND`** |
| `+328` | `D × 0.25` | `bucketKey`'s nearSplit — **and `FOGSTART`** |
| `+332` | `D × 0.95` | `bucketKey`'s farSplit |

The fog ends at `D`, **not** at the 0.95 split — two different numbers, and reading the wrong one is a silent 5%. And the option is a **cap**, not a value: a set's own clip distance is used unless it exceeds the option.

**So the fog is now read rather than guessed** — linear (`FOGTABLEMODE` 3) at density 1.0, coloured from the scene's `+336`, off for key bits `0x2080` (near bucket, transparent) and doubled for `0x800`. That splits step 4 in two: PORTING's "fog has no reachable evidence tier" is about a pixel's *value* and stands, but the fog's existence and parameters are decision-level readable like the blend modes already ported. Step 4 is a renderer change with a written spec now, not research.

**What's wired.** `platform/settings.h` resolves defaults (`sub_41F4C0`) ← `[Preferences]` ← the save header, recording which source supplied each field. Density → `Session::setStreetActivity`; clip distance → a real visible-set walk in `omk-play` (the distance half of `sub_48D3B0`, per set mesh, over corner runs precomputed at load). Anekbah's five choices draw 68 / 242 / 565 / 902 / 1264 mesh runs of 1632, and an absurd distance culls 0.

Two things found by doing it:

- **The density is a spacing**, `39 × (5 − level) × h[3]`, against a 200-walker pool cap — so on Anekbah levels 2/3/4 all saturate and only 0 (138 walkers) differs. Faithful, but the option has less room than its five labels suggest.
- **The clip distance without fog looks wrong** — the second image is the argument for step 4.

I deliberately left out the four side planes: they're the camera's business rather than the option's, and a wrong plane sign deletes the world silently, so the visible set is a superset of the engine's.

`verify.py: settings resolve` added, shown to fail with the precedence inverted and with the fog end read from the 0.95 split. 173 checks, 0 failed — and that includes bumping `licence headers` again for the three new files.

Next is step 3 (does a skybox exist in the data at all) then step 4. Say go and I'll take step 3.

**Claude**

You were right to push, and the honest answer splits in two — **both halves are true**.

**The stored unit really is the inch**, but I was leaning on a constant, which only records what a programmer typed. So I measured something whose size we both already know. `HO1_FNM` is Kay'l's body:

```
extent  X 71.02   Y 70.85   Z 10.49  world units
   as inches → 1.80 m tall     ← a man
   as metres → 70.85 m tall    ← a twenty-storey building
```

The two crowd models check themselves: `PSH_FN` 71.5 and `FSH_FN` 66.7 → **1.82 m and 1.69 m**, a male and a female adult, in the right order. (Their *X* span is four bodies wide because a crowd model carries four LOD skeletons side by side, so only the height measures anything.) No reading makes 70.85 metres a person.

The engine also declares the ratio in both directions, which I should have quoted as corroboration rather than as the proof: `State_Save` writes the player's position as `round(w × 0.0254 × 256)`, and `Area_LoadSet` reads the clip option with `× 39.37007874015748`.

**And you're right about the studio — they worked in metres.** That's visible everywhere, and it's the tell that nobody was thinking in inches:

| constant | world units | metres |
|---|---|---|
| crowd LOD rings | 393.70 / 787.40 / 1181.10 / 1574.80 | **10 / 20 / 30 / 40** |
| vehicle LOD rings | 787.40 / 1181.10 / 1574.80 / 1968.50 | **20 / 30 / 40 / 50** |
| ride camera, eye up / back / target up | 118.1102 / 275.5905 / 78.7402 | **3.00 / 7.00 / 2.00** |
| options row 3 | (converted at use) | **25 / 50 / 100 / 150 / 200** |

Nobody picks 1574.8031 by hand — somebody typed 40 m and something multiplied. Even the two that aren't round metres fit: the crowd's spacing factor and overtake reach are both **39**, an inch-rounded metre (0.991 m), and the walker's gait thresholds 195 and 390 are exactly 5 and 10 of that 39.

So: **the metre is the unit the game was designed in, the inch is the unit it is stored in**, with the conversion at the boundary. That's a property of the modelling tool chain, not of anyone's sense of distance — and it doesn't change step 2's arithmetic, since the conversion was already there.

`verify.py: world unit` now measures the models rather than quoting either constant, and is shown to fail if you read the X span instead of the height, or if you widen the "is this a human" test until the metre reading passes. 174 checks, 0 failed. <cc-memory filenames="back-convention-claims-with-measurements.md">I've noted the general lesson too: back a convention claim with a measurement you can judge without reading code, not with a constant found in it.</cc-memory>

Committed as `2e811ae`, with the reasoning as a box in `docs/ASSETS.md` beside the clip-distance chain — since that's where the next reader will ask the same thing. Not pushed yet; say the word and I'll push, then take step 3.

```c
sprintf(buf, "normale : %f %f %f - Angle : %f - Hauteur : %f meter",
        ..., (v10 - v11) * 0.0254);
```

That's the binary naming the unit **about itself** — it takes a world-space height, multiplies by 0.0254, and labels the result `meter`. Which is this repo's preferred standard for a claim, and I'd missed it.

For completeness on the API question: the one Windows API here that genuinely has units is DirectSound, whose listener takes a distance factor in *metres per world unit*. But that's told the factor, so it adapts to whatever the engine already used — it can't have forced the choice. Pushed, and **step 3 is done — the answer is yes, and it's drawn** (`4c3164a`).

**It's not a skybox or a dome — it's a painted ceiling**, which is what a domed city has. `Area_TickLoad` case 4 hands the AREA chunk's **`+133`** to `Area_LoadMiscModel`, which loads `MESHES\MISC\<name>.3DO`, takes node 0, scales it **12.5×**, lifts it **2250** units, forces it opaque, and sets flag `0x10000`. Then every frame while row 4 is on, the engine puts the node at the **camera's x and z and its own y** — so it slides with you and never approaches.

**The files name themselves**, which beat any inference I could have made: the single 256×256 texture on each is **`ciel1`** or **`ciel2`**, and `toitsky`'s mesh is **`TOITCIEL`** — "roof sky".

**What ships:** 17 of 259 areas name a sky, 242 name none (interiors — which is the check that `+133` is a field and not noise). Six models, all present as `.3DO` + `.3DT`:

| model | areas |
|---|---|
| `ASKY` | Anekbah, Atoit, Qalisar, both Impasses |
| `SSKY` | Jaunpur, Bozzint, the prison, Ruetoit |
| `LSKY` | Lahoreh, Lyrmaly, Lmoexter, Khonsu |
| `MASKY` / `DOCKSKY` / `TOITSKY` | Mahaleel & Sasneige / the docks / the roofs |

A seventh, `jansky`, ships and **nothing names it** — likely more of the cut content you mentioned.

**It's flat.** All seven share identical geometry: 864 corners in a 12×12 grid, every vertex at Y = 401.09, so the Y extent is **exactly zero**. At 12.5× it's 1686 × 1640 m against a clip distance of at most 200 m, so you can never reach its edge.

**One detail that step 4 will need**: `Area_LoadMiscModel` sets mesh flag `0x10000`, and that flag's line in `Render_SubmitMesh` is an *assignment* (`state = 0x800`), not an OR — it wipes every other state bit. So the sky's bucket state is exactly `0x800`, which keeps it out of the far bucket **and doubles its fog range**. One flag, three consequences.

175 checks, 0 failed. `verify.py: the sky` is shown to fail by reading `+134` instead of `+133`, and by making the probe report a non-flat plane. (My first attempt at that second mutation was invalid — I deleted the assertion rather than breaking the data, which of course passed; redone against the probe.)

That leaves **step 4, the fog** — and it's now a renderer change with a written spec rather than research: linear, density 1.0, `clipInches × 0.25` to `clipInches`, colour from the scene's `+336`, off for key bits `0x2080`, doubled for `0x800`. Say go and I'll take it.

**Two lines decode the marketing phrasing into things I'd already found:**
- *"Z-mapping (mapping exact)"* → `D3D_SetRenderState(dev, 4, 1)`, state 4 = `TEXTUREPERSPECTIVE`. It means perspective-correct texturing.
- *"Motion Capture sur des visages temps réel, **entrelacé avec les voix**"* → that is literally the `.3DM` frame record: face vertices, nodes, a `float[3]`, **then the audio**, one record per frame. The format's shape has an explanation now.

Also confirmed: 16-bit/65000 colours is the RGB565 boundary; *"Gouraud mappé"* is the baked `D3DFVF_DIFFUSE` colour plus texture; *"Morph en Motion Blending"* names the two-sided `min(30, frames/4)` slerp; *"Flat/Gouraud Fog"* is step 4.

**One line is partly refuted.** *"Rendu Painter Back to Front et Front to Back"* — `Render_FlushBuckets` walks `0..0x4000` **ascending, once**. Whatever the second order was, it isn't in this build.

**One is not corroborated: the BSP tree.** Nothing here evidences one — the shipped face sort is the 14-bit bucket key, the `.3DO`'s nine header offsets include no tree, and the meshes' parent/child/next is a scene hierarchy. I've written down the three candidate explanations and the test that separates them (byte accounting over a `.3DO`, which doesn't exist yet). Your instinct about marketing copy is probably right here, but it's recorded as open rather than dismissed.

**And the real find — "Multilights" is not marketing.**

```
635 models, 375 of them with lights, 6244 light records, max 470 in Qalisar.3DO
```

`Mesh3doHeader` has carried `lightOff` (+40) and the count (`desc+232`) since the format was decoded, the port fills both — and **nothing has ever read the records**. Far too much data to be vestigial.

The question that opens is the interesting one: the sets are shaded by a colour **baked into every vertex**, so a static set needs no runtime light at all. The obvious candidate for 6244 of them is the thing that *can't* carry baked light — the characters walking through those sets. Nothing establishes that yet, so it's filed as a lead with `verify.py: mesh lights` pinning the numbers.

176 checks, 0 failed.

Two open leads now: the light table, and the BSP question. Both are separate from step 4 — say the word and I'll take **step 4 (the fog)** as planned, or the lights first if that interests you more.

Step 1 done and committed — and it opens by **correcting a number I published a few hours ago**.

**The loader disagreed with the port.** `Read3DO_Init` does:

```c
u32(*v1, 232) = u32(*v1, 240);      /* the count comes from +240 */
if (u32(*v1, 232)) v1[8] = v1[11] + v3[10];
```

It copies `desc+240` over `desc+232` and *then* uses it — so the on-disk `+232`, which is where every other count in that descriptor lives (and why the port read it), never reaches anything. The two fields disagree in **256 of 635** files.

**The corpus settles it independently**, which is what makes this established rather than just re-read. A `.3DO`'s light table is the last thing in the file, so the walk has a size to land on:

| count used | `lightOff + n × 304 == filesize` |
|---|---|
| **`desc+240`** | **216 of 216** |
| `desc+232` | 119 of 216 |

So the record is **304 bytes** and the real figures are **4179 lights across 216 of 635 models**, most being 258 in Qalisar — not the "6244 in 375, max 470" I gave you. Still far too much to be vestigial, so *Multilights* still describes something real; the number was just wrong.

The check now carries the exactness test rather than only the counts, and I re-ran the mutations properly after catching that a stale build had been making them "fail" for the wrong reason — strides 300, 308 and 152 give 0, 0 and 0 exact landings against 304's 216.

The lesson is CLAUDE.md's opening one and it cost a published number: the port's parser is *data* reading, and a field's meaning isn't established until a loader says so. `lights = desc+232` had sat in `mesh3do.cpp` since the format work — plausible, adjacent to every other count, and wrong — and writing a check around it made the error harder to spot, not easier.

`todo/mesh-lights.md` has the four steps. Next is **step 2, decoding the 304-byte record** — I'd expect a position, a colour, a range and a type in there, and 304 bytes is generous enough that there'll be more. Say go and I'll take it.

**Step 3 answered, and it's the hypothesis I'd refused to assert: the set supplies the lights, and the crowd receives them.**

The chain is three functions, and the binary names two of them itself:

1. **`Read3DO_Init`** registers a loaded `.3DO`'s lights into a spatial structure — the failure path says `"unable to add light for Lights Collisions"`. A **decor set supplies the lights**.
2. **`sub_4380B0`** — *LightInstance*, by its own error strings (*"cant light instance - collision buffer full"*) — registers a drawn **instance** in the same structure, handle at `+40`, `-1` meaning unlit. Its eight call sites are six functions and **every one is street-life**: the walker and vehicle spawn callbacks, the walker tick, two from `Slider_Init`, and the player's ride mount.
3. **`sub_48D7F0`**, the draw walk, closes it: per mesh, if the handle isn't `-1` it queries which lights overlap, transforms each **once** a frame, applies it, and only then submits.

**And the lighting maths traces another field.** `sub_493E40` is per-vertex:

```
d = mesh.pos − light.transformedPos              (light +60)
if |d|² <= light[+240]              the OUTER radius SQUARED, cached at load
    k  = light[+32] × 256
    k *= 1 − (|d| − light[+28]) / (light[+24] − light[+28])    LINEAR falloff
    per vertex: t = −(N · L)        L = light +124, into the mesh's frame
    if t > 0: vertex.rgb += ramp[light[+44,+45,+46]][t]        saturating
```

So **`+32` is the intensity**, closing one of the three floats step 2 left unexplained — `+36` and `+40` are untouched here and stay open. The three colour bytes index 256-entry ramps, and the add goes through a saturating table: how a palette-era engine adds light without a multiply per channel.

Two corrections on the way, both from the check disagreeing with me: there are **six** caller functions, not the five I'd sampled by hand (`sub_4541E0`, reached from `Slider_Init`), and I had the expected list in the wrong sort order.

`verify.py: light consumers` asserts the exact offsets `sub_493E40` reads and that no LightInstance caller sits outside the street-life module; shown to fail by reading the mesh's offsets instead of the light's. 179 checks, 0 failed.

That leaves **step 4 — porting it.** It's well-specified now, and the payoff is visible: Anekbah's 155 lights would fall on the walkers and vehicles you saw in the demo, which currently draw with flat baked colour. Say go and I'll take it, and there'll be a before/after to look at.

While waiting I've committed the two traps to `CLAUDE.md` (`84f8972`), because both are the kind that make a check *pass* when it shouldn't:

- **The zsh word-splitting note gains its recurrences.** It happened three times today despite the existing paragraph, so I've written down the three *symptoms* — the symptom is what you meet first and it's never "bad argument". `set -- $tag` put the flags in the output *filename* and produced three identical renders (a comparison with nothing compared); `omk-play $B` passed one giant argument and the program waited, which read as a slow render for fourteen minutes. The rule that catches all three: **after building a command from a variable, assert the effect, not the exit status** — a render that produced no file, three identical dumps, and a mutation that changed nothing all exit 0.

- **A new ground rule**: a mutation that doesn't apply passes, and a stale object file passes. `PORTING` B2 requires every check to be *shown* to fail, and four mutations "passed" today for reasons unrelated to the check — two anchors that didn't match, one `find A -o B | xargs rm` deleting only B (`-print` binds to the last expression, so the object survived and the rebuild relinked it), and one probe too weak to tell the right value from the wrong one.

That last one is the one I'd most want a future session to have: a check that can't distinguish right from wrong is worse than no check, because it reports confidence.

> `Bash` — Read the baseline comparison


**Claude**

**It is mine** — the base gives **9095** pixels (comfortably over the 3000 threshold, so it passed) against today's 1104. My attribution was right; both my *explanations* were wrong. The remaining change I hadn't considered: my settings work made the crowd **density come from the save (4)** where it used to default to **3**, which rearranges the walkers entirely:

> `Bash` — Test whether the density change is the cause


**Claude**

**Confirmed mine: base 9095 pixels, today 1104.** So my original attribution was right and both of my explanations were wrong — which is worth separating, because "I broke it" and "here's why" are different claims and I'd been treating the second as following from the first.

The candidate I'd overlooked until now is the one that actually changes *what is in the frame* rather than what colour it is: **my settings layer made the crowd density come from the save's header (4) where the viewer previously defaulted to 3**. Density is a *spacing* — `39 × (5 − level) × h[3]` — so 4 packs the walkers at half the spacing of 3 and, after 700 frames, an entirely different set of them is near the camera. That would move the pixel count without any colour changing, which fits every measurement so far: lighting off/on identical, fog off/on identical.

The test is running. If density 3 restores ~9095, the fix isn't to touch the threshold at all — it's that **the check must pin the density it was calibrated at**, since it now inherits whatever the save says. That's a better outcome than a re-baseline: the check would have been silently measuring a different scene every time the save's settings changed.

**Reproduced exactly, and it's linear in hold time:**

```
hold Enter  1 frame  -> 1 action
hold Enter 15 frames -> 15 actions     (your 0.5 s)
hold Enter 30 frames -> 30 actions
```

**It is not the repeat mask.** The port already implements the engine's rule faithfully — `edges = held & (held ^ (repeatMask & last))`, `0x203F` under a screen and `0` in the world — and the dialogue confirm already takes `edgeBits`. My triage note was wrong about where to look.

**The real path**: the `.CTL` channel never changes state. It sits in `0 'H_STAND'` with the idle clip advancing, while a *per-tick* entry emits the `MDACTION` special move every frame:

```
per-tick move 'MDACTION'  from 0 to 24  flags 0xC5F00013
                          inputCode 0x10  word 0x10  lastInput 0x0
```

The engine *does* have a held-button guard — `if (word != lastInput_) commit` — transcribed from `Cef_TickChannel` 0x004A853A. But this entry's flags contain **`0x10000000`, whose only job is `lastInput_ = 0`**. It clears the guard every tick, so the same held word commits again next tick and the move re-fires. The port's comment asserting *"entering a state is once per press by construction"* is false here: no state is ever entered.

**The open question, and it decides the fix**: what input word the engine hands the channel. `Cef_TickChannel` takes only the channel pointer, so the word is set on it beforehand — and I haven't yet found that writer. If the engine feeds the actor an *edged* word, the port's world-side mask of 0 is the bug. If it feeds a held word, then the guard against re-firing must live in `tab_special_move[3]`'s MDACTION handler (0x0046AEC0), and that's what needs transcribing.

I'd rather find that writer than guess between those two — it's the difference between a one-line mask change and porting a handler. Shall I carry on?

> `Bash` — cd /Users/sofianekerrakchou/Documents/omikron/claude/omk python3 - <<'PY' p='todo/next-tasks.md'; s=open(p).r…


**Claude**

Found the writer, and it settled the question — but not the way either branch predicted.

**The engine feeds the channel a *held* word, same as the port.** `Cef_TickChannel` reads input via `sub_43E080` → `sub_43D920`, which is a DirectInput **state** read (bit `0x80` = key down), and `sub_4A7A20(channel, raw, &word)` is a pure bit remapper onto the 14 slots with a left/right mirror under channel flag 8. **No edge logic anywhere.** The port's per-tick loop even passes the same `Cef_FindTransition(…, 2, 784, 1)` filters.

So the original **also** queues `MDACTION` every frame while Enter is held. My "one-line mask change" branch is dead.

**The guard is in the handler, and the port never runs it.** `tab_special_move[3]` = **0x0046AEC0** (no `proc` label — the documented trap, read via `asmfn.py`). It switches on `[esi+194h]`, the actor's **`+404` ACTOR_STATE**, over 11 cases; states 5–10 and 12 fall to a default arm that:

- searches for the nearest interactable (`sub_41C810`) and **returns if it found nothing**;
- returns if **ACTOR_STATE == 3**;
- applies a distance test against `flt_4BC918`;
- branches on the actor's `+164`, and only then reaches `sub_465D30(actor, obj, 0)` — the function `play.cpp` already names in a comment.

`omk-play` today fires `session.pressAction()` for **every** `MDACTION` name with none of that gating. That's the bug.

**The fix is to transcribe 0x0046AEC0** — explicitly *not* an edge filter or a hold timer. Both would invent a rule the engine hasn't got, and a timer would have hidden the real one permanently. I've revised the item **S → M** in `todo/next-tasks.md`: the mechanism is fully understood, but the work is a state-gated handler and its object search, not a mask.

Committed as the diagnosis only (`8569acb`) — no code changed yet. Worth noting my triage's confident "mechanism already documented, fix is to filter" was wrong on both counts; the reproduction is what corrected it.

Pushed. Continuing turned up the thing that reframes this item: **it isn't an input bug.**

Reading `sub_465D30` through, it's in three parts:

1. **The adjust** (its first two thirds) — the actor→object vector, the actor's facing, the signed angle between them, then `Actor_Move` to stand **0.600 m** from a low object or **0.400 m** from a standing one, with a 0.25 m settle tolerance and a 1.25 m give-up. Round metres again.
2. **The bank switch** (its tail) — `Cef_FindGroupById(bank, dy <= 27.472441 ? 143 : 41)`. This is the repeat guard, and it's *one line*.
3. **The way back** — and this is why the switch alone strands the player. Group 41's default entry lands in `.CTL` state **54 `H_WAITOB`** — *wait for object*. Its only two exits are states 55 and 56: flags `0x80000013`, **no clip**, both carrying the move bit. They're entries waiting on the take pipeline's own special move. **The actor leaves `H_WAITOB` when the object is delivered, and nothing else does it.**

So the order has to be **3 before 2**: port enough of the take for `H_WAITOB` to have an exit, and the bank switch then closes the repeat for free. Doing 2 first is measurably worse than the bug — which I proved by doing it and parking the player permanently.

**The conclusion worth carrying**: the Enter-repeat symptom is a *consequence of the take being unported*, not an input fault. Someone spending a day on input code would find nothing — the triage's "S, strong evidence, fix is to filter" was wrong at every level, and only the reproduction and then the read corrected it. I've revised the item to **M/L** and written the decomposition into `todo/next-tasks.md`.

That's a natural stopping point for this item: it's now a well-specified slice ("port the world take") rather than a bug with an unknown cause. I'd rather start that fresh than open a 190-line geometry port at the end of a long session — but say the word and I'll take it, or move to another item on the list.

Noted on the shop sellers — recorded in the plan file, not yet reproduced, and it may well be the same root cause seen from the other side.

Two corrections to what I told you last message, both from reading the data instead of reasoning about it:

**1. The take is much smaller than I said.** "The actor leaves `H_WAITOB` when the object is delivered" was wrong. It exits on **player input**:

| state | group | input | move |
|---|---|---|---|
| 24 | 0 | `0x10` Enter | `MDACTION` |
| 52 | 3 | — | `MDGETOBJ` *(automatic)* |
| **54 `H_WAITOB`** | 4 | — | the wait pose |
| **55** | 4 | **`0x10` Enter** | `MDPUTSNK` — take it |
| **56** | 4 | **`0x20` Space** | `MDNOTAKE` — decline |

So the original is **two presses**: Enter to reach, Enter again to take or Space to decline. And holding doesn't repeat because after the first press the machine is in group 4, where the same bit means *take*, not *reach*. That's elegant, and it's not a 190-line geometry port.

**2. The port already gets most of the way.** With the bank switch applied, `MDACTION` fires **once**, `MDGETOBJ` follows automatically, the actor reaches `H_WAITOB`, and `findTransition(54, 0x10)` **finds state 55**. The chain is correct.

**The blocker is the frontend, not the take.** In `H_WAITOB` the channel receives `0x40000000` (idle) every tick with Enter held for 60 frames, and `play.cpp`'s own `bits` is `0000` at the same instant. The suspect is:

```cpp
if (walk) in.setRepeatMask(kUiRepeatMask);
else if (adventure) in.setRepeatMask(0);
```

With **neither** true — which is what a running zone script looks like — the mask keeps its last value, and after the boot screens that's `0x203F`. The world then gets *edges* where it should get held bits. That's one-line-shaped, and it's the next thing to test.

Which makes your shop-seller report interesting: that's the same gating from the other side — a screen whose mask may be left at the world's 0.

Tree is clean, experiment backed out, checks green, committed as `b0431d1`. Next step is testing that mask hypothesis — shall I?

**Fixed and pushed.** Enter held for 1, 15, 30 or 60 frames now gives **one** activation each, where it was one per frame.

**It was never an input bug.** The repeat mask, the dialogue edge filter, and the world's mask of 0 were all checked and all innocent. The engine reads DirectInput *state* and `sub_4A7A20` is a pure bit remap, so the original also queues `MDACTION` every frame while the button is down.

The guard is `sub_465D30`'s tail: `SetPersoBankGroup` switches the actor into the take bank, where the same held bit means **take** rather than **reach** — so the machine walks the interaction instead of repeating its first step:

```
MDACTION → MDGETOBJ → [H_WAITOB] → MDPUTSNK → MDSTAND → a new cycle
```

40 frames reaches and waits, 55 completes the take, 70+ completes it and starts a new cycle — which is what holding *should* do. Two separate presses give two activations. A press with nothing in reach still retries every frame, which is also the engine, since `sub_465D30` is never reached.

Still unported and labelled at the call site: the **low arm** (group 143, when the object is within 0.6978 m of the actor's height — needs the object position the press doesn't carry), and `sub_465D30`'s **adjust** (the walk to 0.600/0.400 m).

**The part I'd flag hardest**: I published two confident conclusions on the way — *"the bank switch parks the player in `H_WAITOB` for ever"* and *"the actor only leaves when the object is delivered"* — and **both were wrong for the same reason**. `--hold` takes comma-separated runs and uses `+` only for simultaneous keys, so `k28*5+0*10+k28*60` parsed as Enter for **five frames**. Every "second press" test pressed nothing. That caused a back-out of a correct fix and a wrong entry in the plan file.

It's also the exact failure I wrote into `CLAUDE.md` earlier today — *assert the effect, not the exit status* — and I then walked into it twice more. Worth knowing that writing the rule down didn't stop me applying it.

Your shop-seller report is recorded and now worth re-testing: if that path also runs through `MDACTION`, this may have fixed it too.

**Claude**

Yes — sorry for the silence. The bug is found and fixed; I've been verifying. Where it stands:

**What was wrong — three faults, not one.**

1. **The bank switch was made on the wrong condition.** `sub_465D30` reaches `SetPersoBankGroup` only when MDACTION's object scan *found* something. The port switched into the take bank (group 41, whose entry is `H_TAKL12`) on **any** successful press — so activating a talk zone installed the take bank and played the whole reach → wait → put-back graph on nothing. That is exactly what you saw. The real object path was already ported 300 lines above and installs 41/143/600 itself, so the extra switch is gone.

2. **The viewer never entered or left dialogue mode.** `Actor_EnterDialogueMode` (`0x00468DE0`) and its counterpart at `0x00468E80` bracket every conversation and both switch the player's `.CTL` bank — group 400 on the way in, group 100 (H1AVNT's default group, entry `H_STAND`/`MDSTAND`) on the way out. Both have been ported since the ACTOR_STATE work and **nothing ever called them**.

3. **`Perso_SetInputEnabled` is misnamed, and the port believed the name.** Flag `0x80` *blocks* the device pass. Wiring (2) in on its own therefore made things worse — the action button died permanently after any conversation — until I read the arms: `or cl, 80h` for argument 1, `and al, 7Fh` plus a reset for argument 0, and `Perso_GetInputEnabled` returns `flags & 0x80` precisely so the leave can **restore** what the enter saved. Renamed `setInputBlocked` in the port.

**Measured, headless:**

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| 100-frame Enter hold at a zone | 1 activation, 1 take move | 1 activation, **0 take moves** |
| shop seller conversation | take graph on the last Enter | runs once, ends into screen 27, no take |

One transcription fix found on the way that I am **not** claiming caused this: neither of the engine's channel resets clears the 20-slot latch — both stores are 16 dwords from +28, ending at +87, and the latch is at +92, one dword past. Corrected, but on H1AVNT it changes nothing observable.

Two harness faults fixed too: `--hold` was swallowed while a conversation was open (so no headless run could ever reach the end of one), and the zsh word-splitting trap hit a fourth time and zeroed a whole round of measurements.

`verify.py: dialogue mode` is new and shown to fail — asserting the block flag instead of restoring it takes `MDACTION after leaving` from 8 to 0. The full `--slow` sweep is running now; I'll commit and hand you a live build once it's clean.

- **`CLAUDE.md` §1** — a new extraction trap (a name in `tools/renames.json` is a hypothesis, not evidence; read the handler, not the map — worst with a boolean argument), and the zsh word-splitting list gains its fourth case of the day.
